Its guards are all real cases: still in-game, still socket-less (they may have returned between the alarm being set and firing), a seat that still maps, and the engine's own refusal if the game finished first — so a forfeit racing a genuine win changes nothing. Deadlines are consumed whether or not the forfeit lands, so a stale entry can't re-fire every alarm. **`SUPPORTED_MATCH_ACTIONS` += `concede`**, serving both triggers at once; it is the safest entry on that set — no wire values at all beyond the seat the DO binds itself — and a griefer gains nothing, since the only game they can throw away is their own. **Two real bugs caught while building, both invisible to tests:** (1) the abandon deadlines were persisted but NOT added to `scheduleMaintenance`, so nothing would ever wake the DO to fire them — the arming alarm consumes its own pending entry, and a lobby with one live socket never reaches the empty-lobby TTL, so the feature would have silently never worked; the scheduler's comment now says why that line is load-bearing. (2) a first-draft change check compared array REFERENCES against a fresh storage read, which differ every time, so it would have written on every alarm — replaced with an explicit flag. **Client:** both game-over overlays now show WHY the game ended. The local one's reason chain and the engine log's were ternary chains whose final `else` would have rendered `conceded` as "the opponent could not draw"; both became TOTAL `Record`s (`GAME_OVER_DETAIL` in projection.ts, `GAME_OVER_REASONS` in log.ts), so a future reason is a compile error. The online overlay previously showed no reason at all — a bare "You win!" would now leave a forfeit unexplained — and it shares `GAME_OVER_DETAIL` with the local one rather than restating it, because the D60/D62/D63 restate-per-surface rule is about MARKUP (which genuinely differs) while this is a shape-neutral string map — the `EnergyDots` case — and two screens disagreeing about what just happened would be a bug. 1825 → **1832** tests (concede from any phase incl. the opponent's turn, the cleared pending queue, the refusal once over; the DO's forfeit seam through `applyMatchAction` — outcome, log growth, still-readable record, seat binding beating a forged payload seat, and the finished-match race). `bun run check` green. **NOT yet built (3c-iii):** the client Concede BUTTON — the action and its allowlist entry are in place, so it is one dialog away; and the opponent still gets no countdown ("forfeits in 0:45"), which would need the deadline on the wire. **BROWSER-VERIFIED end to end** (2026-07-26, two real clients): with a match live, the guest's browser was killed outright and the host — who touched nothing — was shown "You win! / The match was forfeited." ~95s later, with the system log row to match. That run is the only proof that exists for the `scheduleMaintenance` fix above: had the abandon deadlines stayed unscheduled, nothing would have woken the DO and the host would still be sitting there. *Why: applying the engine's own concede rather than inventing a server-side "abandoned" state means there is exactly one way a match ends, and every consumer already handles it. Separating the two clocks is the crux — a 5s forfeit would be indefensible and a never-resolving lobby is the actual bug — and 90s is long enough to survive a tab reload or a phone changing networks while short enough that the abandoned player isn't held hostage. Keeping one `conceded` reason rather than adding a distinct "disconnected" one keeps the engine ignorant of transport, which is the boundary that has held all through P4.* (2026-07-26) |
| D74 | The Concede control, and neutral game-over copy (P4 online, increment 3c-iii) | 3c-ii gave the SERVER a way to end an abandoned match but left the player without one: you could be forfeited, you could not forfeit. This adds the control. **Placement is the decision:** it sits in the HUD chrome beside the playmat's own "Back" (top-right, `right: 96` to its `16`), NOT in the turn panel — because that panel renders only on YOUR turn, while the engine deliberately allows conceding in any phase, and the case that matters most is precisely the one the panel can't serve: waiting out an opponent who has walked away on THEIR turn. It hides once `outcome !== null` (nothing left to concede, and the engine would refuse it). An irreversible action gets the existing `ConfirmDialog` with `danger` styling. **Browser-verified end to end** on two real clients: the button hit-tests clickable on both (the collision check the Pass-turn bug taught), and a concede during SETUP — turn 0, before the coin flip is even answered — ended the match for both, proving the engine's any-phase legality is real rather than a unit-test artifact: the conceder saw "<opponent> wins", the winner "You win!", and both logs carried the system row. **A copy bug the browser run caught, which tests could not:** the game-over detail line was winner-centric ("The opponent forfeited."), and the online overlay shows the SAME line to both players — so the conceding player was told their opponent forfeited. It was already ambiguous on the local hot-seat overlay, which belongs to neither player; only routing the reason to a per-viewer online overlay in 3c-ii made it a visible lie. Fixed by making `GAME_OVER_DETAIL` possessive-free — "No Pokémon left in play.", "No cards left to draw.", "The match was forfeited." — which is accurate from either side and needs no viewer-relative variant, since each overlay's TITLE already names the winner. (A viewer-relative map — "You forfeited." vs "Your opponent forfeited." — would read slightly better and is a cheap later polish; it was rejected here as 12 strings and a perspective argument for a line that sits under an unambiguous title.) 1832 → **1835** tests (confirm-then-dispatch with a cancel that dispatches nothing, the control offered on the OPPONENT's turn — the case it exists for — and its disappearance once the game is over, which also pins the neutral copy). `bun run check` green. **Still open:** an explicit LEAVE (`bye`) during a match still VOIDS it rather than conceding — the opponent's game simply vanishes with no result — which is now the odd one out beside both the timeout forfeit and this button; recorded, not changed, because making `bye` concede first raises a real question about whether the leaver stays seated long enough for the other player to see the result. *Why: putting the control in the chrome rather than the turn panel follows the engine's own any-phase rule instead of fighting it, and keeps the one irreversible action away from the buttons a player clicks in a hurry. Neutral copy over per-viewer copy is the smaller correct fix: the bug was a claim about WHO acted, and the cheapest way to stop making a false claim is to stop making the claim.* (2026-07-26) |

| D75 | Walking out of a running match FORFEITS it, and an in-game `bye` never frees the seat (P4 online, increment 3c-iv) | The last way an online match could end with no result. A `bye` — the frame every leave sends, from the Back control's confirm and from `useLobby`'s unmount alike — took the pre-P4 path: a guest's freed the seat, which drops the lobby out of in-game, which makes `commit` delete `MATCH_KEY`, so the opponent's board and game simply VANISHED mid-play with nothing said; a host's marked their seat away and left the guest to wait out the full 5s + 90s abandonment clock for a player who had explicitly said goodbye. Now the DO CONCEDES for the leaver first (a new private **`concedeSeat(snapshot, seat, now)`** — extracted from `forfeitAbandoned`, so the graceful leave and the 90s timeout are literally the same code path and produce the same `GameOutcome`, log row and overlay as a pressed Concede), and only then commits the lobby change. **The seat-keeping half is the real decision, and it answers the question 3c-iii deferred** ("does the leaver stay seated long enough for the other player to SEE the result?"): a new pure **`applyBye(snapshot, slot)`** (logic.ts) keeps the seat for BOTH slots while the lobby is in-game and falls back to the old free-the-guest / park-the-host semantics otherwise. Freeing the seat would have undone the concede a frame after it landed — same `abandonsMatch` → record-delete → blank board — so the concede would have been pointless. It deliberately covers a FINISHED game too, for two reasons: a leave is often TWO `bye` frames (an explicit `leave()` plus the unmount's), and under a live-only rule the second would have voided what the first correctly ended; and the loser clicking Back after the overlay would have yanked the winner's own result off screen. The cost is that a lobby which has been in-game has no way back to `selecting` except the host's kick — accepted, because **rematch** is the next slice and `endMatch` (D72) is already the primitive for it, and because "your board vanished" is a worse failure than "this room is finished". **Client:** the Back confirmation now tells the truth before it acts — `BackToMenu` takes an overridable `LeaveConfirmCopy` (default = the local /play wording, forwarded through `PlaymatView.leaveConfirm`) and the online match sets it to "Leaving forfeits the match — your opponent wins immediately." while the game is live, dropping to a plain goodbye once it is over. Silently converting "Back" into "you lose" was the one part of this change a player must never meet unannounced. 1835 → **1841** tests. `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two real clients, in BOTH directions** — necessary because the DO class has no tests at all (`applyBye` is unit-tested; the `bye` → `concedeSeat` wiring is not): the host leaving and the guest leaving each ended the match instantly for the player who stayed, who kept their board, got "You win! / The match was forfeited." and the `<winner> wins — the opponent forfeited` log row, and — the guest case, the void this closes — was never dropped to the "Waiting for an opponent" screen with the game gone. Real hit-tested clicks on Back (not a synthetic `.click()`), the forfeit warning showing on both, zero page errors; both runs left during SETUP, re-confirming D74's any-phase concede legality. **Not yet:** the opponent-facing "forfeits in 0:45" countdown for an UNGRACEFUL drop (the 3c-ii path, which is now the only one that waits) — it needs the deadline on the wire. *Why: reusing the engine's own concede for every way a match can end — pressed, timed out, walked out — keeps ONE definition of "the match is over" that every consumer already handles, and the seat-keeping rule follows from it: a result nobody is left to see is not a result. Making the in-game rule total (live or finished, host or guest) is what makes it safe against the double-`bye` the client actually sends, rather than correct only in the case we happened to test.* (2026-07-26) |

| D76 | The forfeit deadline lives on the SEAT, and the opponent can see it count down (P4 online, increment 3c-v) | 3c-ii could forfeit an abandoned player and 3c-iv made a graceful leave do it instantly, but the one path that still WAITS — an ungraceful drop (crash, killed tab, dead network) — waited in total silence: the player still there watched a motionless board for 90 seconds and was then handed a win with no explanation of what had just been decided. **The decision is where the deadline lives.** It moved out of the DO's own `ABANDON_KEY` storage list and onto **`PlayerState.forfeitAt`** in the lobby snapshot, which is a wire type — so the number the opponent's countdown renders is literally the number the alarm fires on, and the two cannot drift. The snapshot was the right carrier rather than the `match` frame for three converging reasons: the arming moment (a disconnect grace expiring in `alarm()`) already commits a snapshot and sends no match frame; a forfeit is lobby/transport state that the engine knows nothing about; and a reconnecting client gets it for free with the state it already receives. Mirroring it into the snapshot while `ABANDON_KEY` stayed authoritative was rejected outright — two sources for one deadline is exactly how a countdown ends up promising something the server won't do. **The invariant that makes it safe is `connected` ⇒ `forfeitAt === null`**, enforced by making `setSeatConnected` take the deadline as its final argument (defaulting to null, so you cannot write one without deciding the other) and by clearing it in `seatGuest` / `reseatHost` / `makePlayer` — the three ways a seat is marked present. That REPLACED D73's `clearDisconnectTimers`, whose whole reason for existing was the fear that a future branch would cancel one timer and leave the other armed: with the deadline on the seat, "mark them present" and "don't forfeit them" became a single indivisible write instead of two calls. `forfeitAbandoned` dissolved into the alarm, which now judges due deadlines on the FINAL snapshot and consumes them in the same commit. **A second, quieter decision: `readSnapshot`.** `SNAPSHOT_KEY` has no version gate, so adding a field would have made every in-flight lobby unreadable across the deploy; the snapshot is now read through a normalizer that fills fields added since it was written. That is the OPPOSITE of D72's answer for `MatchRecord`, deliberately: a lobby snapshot is a handful of scalars with no embedded engine state, so a missing field has exactly one sensible value and the cost of being wrong is cosmetic — whereas a `GameState` from a build whose rules moved reads back "fine" and plays wrong. The guard is on the field's TYPE, not on nullishness, because it feeds `Math.min` in the alarm scheduler and an `undefined` would set the next alarm to NaN. **Client:** a top-centre `OpponentAwayBanner` — "Opp disconnected — forfeits in 1:30", ticking; "forfeiting…" past zero rather than a frozen 0:00, because the server's alarm is what actually ends it and may be a moment behind; "waiting for them to reconnect…" when no clock is armed. Hidden once the game is over. 1841 → **1853** tests, `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two real clients, all three paths:** killing a client outright brought the banner up at +5s with a full 1:30, counted it down live, and fired the forfeit at zero ("You win! / The match was forfeited.") — which is also the proof that moving the deadline did not break the scheduling that D73 nearly shipped broken; and a genuine reconnect (same tab navigated away and back, so the playerId survived) cleared the banner at once and left the match running well past the original deadline, i.e. the returning player was NOT forfeited. Zero page errors. **The run also exposed a PRE-EXISTING gap, recorded not fixed:** a HALF-OPEN socket is invisible to the server — `setWebSocketAutoResponse` answers keepalives without waking the DO and nothing watches for pings that stop arriving, so a player whose network dies without a TCP close is never marked disconnected and never forfeits. Every ordinary way of leaving (tab close, browser kill, navigation, reload, crash) does produce a close event, which is why this has never bitten; closing it needs server-side liveness (a per-socket `lastSeen` instead of the auto-response, or an idle sweep) and is its own slice. *Why: a countdown is a promise about what the server will do, so it must read the server's own number — putting the deadline on the wire type that the alarm consults is the only shape where that is true by construction rather than by discipline. And folding the cancel into the same write as "they're back" turns the scariest failure mode here — forfeiting a player who returned — from a thing you have to remember into a thing you cannot express.* (2026-07-26) |

| D77 | Rematch is a lifecycle FRAME that ends the match and readies the asker (P4 online, increment 3c-vi) | The hole 3c-iv opened on purpose: since a mid-match `bye` keeps the seat, an in-game lobby had no route back to `selecting` except the host's kick, so two players who had just finished a game were forced to mint a new code to play again. **Three shapes were on the table, and the middle one won.** A mutual OFFER/ACCEPT handshake ("waiting for them to accept…") means a second piece of lobby state to persist, expire and reconcile, and a screen that can wedge if the answer never comes. A plain `endMatch` that drops both players on the versus screen to re-ready is correct but makes the asker press twice and records nothing about who asked. The shipped shape — **`endMatch` + ready the asker**, one commit — reuses the ready-up flow VERBATIM as the agreement mechanism: the countdown still starts only when both seats are ready, so pressing Rematch *is* an offer, readying *is* the acceptance, and un-readying on the versus screen *is* the withdrawal, all through machinery that already existed and is already tested. The pure helper is literally `applyIntent(endMatch(snapshot), slot, {type: "set-ready", ready: true})` — routing through `applyIntent` rather than writing `ready: true` keeps "you can't be ready without a deck" in one place, so a player whose saved deck vanished between games lands back un-readied instead of in an impossible state. **It is a `{kind: "rematch", from}` client FRAME, not a `LobbyIntent` member**, because its legality is not a property of the snapshot the shared reducer sees: only the DO can tell whether the match it holds has actually reached `gameOver`, and a rematch during a LIVE game must be refused (conceding is how you leave one of those). The DO gates on seated + bound socket + `in-game` + a readable record whose `state.phase.kind === "gameOver"`, retiring a stale record on the way past. `commit` then sees the lobby leave in-game, `abandonsMatch` fires and `MATCH_KEY` is dropped — which is exactly what makes the next handoff mint a FRESH game instead of hitting `handoffToMatch`'s idempotency guard on the game that just ended. **Client:** the button lives INSIDE the game-over result panel (which is `pointer-events-none` so the finished board stays inspectable; the button opts back in), styled as the one primary action on the board rather than more HUD glass, because this is the one moment it applies. 1853 → **1858** tests, `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two real clients, including the griefing guard:** mid-game neither board offered it; after a concede both did; pressing it on one returned BOTH to the versus screen with decks kept, the asker readied and the opponent showing "Ready" on the other side; the opponent readying started a genuinely NEW game — the log restarted at T0 with fresh shuffles and a new coin flip, proving the record was dropped rather than re-served. And a CRAFTED mid-game `rematch`, sent over a second socket properly bound to a player's own id and account, was silently dropped with the live board untouched. Zero page errors. *Why: the cheapest correct feature is the one that adds no new state — "ready" already means "I want to play this game", so a rematch is just that flow re-entered, and the only genuinely new thing is the server-side judgement of whether the match is over, which is exactly why it is a frame and not an intent.* (2026-07-26) |

| D78 | The spectator view is `redactGame(..., spectating)` — one gate, both sides symmetric (P4 online, increment 3c-vii-a) | Spectate is the last item on the P4 plan, and it splits cleanly: this is the HIDDEN-INFORMATION half, landed on its own so it can be reviewed as a security change rather than buried in transport and UI (the 2b-iii-a precedent — the seam before its consumer). **The threat model is the decision.** A spectator is not a stranger: the realistic attacker is a PLAYER who opens a spectator view of the match they are playing to read the other hand. So the property is "a spectator sees strictly LESS than either seated player", and the two sides are treated symmetrically — `redactGame(state, side, true)` withholds BOTH hands, BOTH sides' setup placements (a seated viewer sees their own because they placed them; a spectator placed nothing, so watching from p1's side must not reveal what p1 benched) and every actor affordance. `viewerSeat` degrades from "who I am" to "which side renders at the bottom". **The mechanism matters as much as the rule:** the five `turn:action` affordance helpers already guarded on `viewerSeat !== turnSeat`, so widening that parameter to `Seat | null` and passing ONE `actor = spectating ? null : phase.seat` withholds all five through guards that already existed — rather than five `spectating ? [] : …` branches that the next affordance added there could silently miss. That is not tidiness: `trainers`/`abilities`/`rareCandy` name cards **in the actor's hand**, so leaking them would undo the hand redaction two lines above. The `effect:choose` prompt is withheld the same way (its candidates can be the answerer's own deck cards, and a spectator can never answer). What still crosses is exactly what is public at a real table: both boards after setup, the Stadium, discards, prize/deck COUNTS, hand SIZES, whose turn it is and the outcome. Alternatives rejected: a separate `redactForSpectator` that post-processes a seated snapshot (fragile — a new leaky field passes straight through unless someone remembers to strip it), and letting a spectator see both hands behind a "trusted spectator" flag (there is no such thing when either player can open the link). engine RULES UNCHANGED (0.41.0 — a read surface, not a rules surface; `MATCH_RECORD_VERSION` untouched since neither `GameState` nor the record moved). 1858 → **1865** tests, including the one that matters: no uid private to EITHER seat appears anywhere in a spectator snapshot, asserted from both sides of the table. **NOT WIRED YET** — nothing calls it until 3c-vii-b, which is deliberately scoped to reuse the lobby link as the spectate link: a third socket whose `hello` is `reject-full` becomes a spectator IF the lobby is in-game (you can watch a match, you cannot watch a lobby), which needs no new frame and no new URL. *Why: the safe default has to be structural. Anyone can add a field to `RedactedPhase` next month; the only way that field is withheld from a spectator by default is if the withholding lives at the one place the actor is decided, not at each field.* (2026-07-26) |

| D79 | The lobby link IS the spectate link — a refused seat becomes a spectator (P4 online, increment 3c-vii-b) | Wiring 3c-vii-a's seatless redaction to real viewers, with the design constraint that it add **no new frame, no new URL and no invite flow**. The entry point is a refusal that already existed: `helloDecision` returns `reject-full` to a third player, and that branch now — **only while the lobby is `in-game`** — also marks the socket a spectator (`attachSpectator`, attachment.ts) and hands it the snapshot plus `redactGame(state, "p1", true)`. The rule falls out of the code rather than being imposed on it: **you can watch a MATCH, you cannot watch a lobby.** Outside in-game the refusal stays the dead end it has always been, which is right — a pre-match lobby has nothing to show, and a seat may yet free up for that person. **A spectator is deliberately not a participant in any sense the lobby can see:** no `playerId`, no `PlayerState`, nothing in the snapshot, so no rule anywhere depends on how many are watching, and the reducer is untouched. Their safety is structural twice over — every handler that can change the game gates on `attachedPlayer`, which stays null for them, and the board they receive is the one that withholds both hands. `attachPlayer` clears the flag, so a watcher who later takes a freed seat stops getting the seatless board. **Players are NOT told they are being watched** — chosen deliberately: telling them needs a spectator count on the wire and a broadcast on every join/leave, and the lobby already treats a refused socket silently. Recorded here so the next session can reverse it knowingly rather than discover it. **Deliberate consequence:** anyone with (or guessing) a 4-character lobby code can watch a game in progress. What they get is a public table — both boards, no hands, no deck order, no prizes — which is the intended product behaviour, not an oversight. **Client:** `useLobby`'s sticky `rejected` boolean became `refusal: "full" | "kicked" | null`, because the two are no longer the same thing — a KICK is terminal, while "full" keeps the connection, keeps accepting `state` (for the players' names) and `match` (the board), and flips the status to `spectating` on the first board. Its hello retry moved to the idle-driven cadence heartbeats already keep fresh, so a spectator whose socket drops re-announces and is re-marked, without becoming a retry storm against a lobby that is simply full. `OnlineMatch` gained `spectating`, which removes every control (`OnlineHud`, Concede, Rematch, drags, Pass, the forfeit banner), keeps the board, the LOG and the result, and asks a leave question that costs nothing. 1865 → **1874** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED with a third, signed-out profile on the same lobby link:** the board rendered; at `setup:place` the player saw their own hand as "Skeledirge; Skeledirge; Rare Candy; …" while the spectator saw "Face-down card" ×7 for BOTH hands — the hidden-information property, live; the spectator had no Concede/Ready/Rematch and a disabled Pass; a concede propagated to them instantly with the result but no Rematch; a rematch dropped them back to "Lobby is full" (the watch-a-match-not-a-lobby rule); and they picked the NEXT match up automatically when it started. Zero page errors on all three. **The run caught a real bug:** `state` frames only go out on a lobby COMMIT, so a spectator joining mid-match never received one and their board read "Host vs Guest" — `refuseSeat` now sends the snapshot explicitly. *Why: the cheapest correct entry point is one that already exists. "Refused a seat" and "may watch" are the same fact about a socket, so deriving one from the other costs a branch instead of a protocol, and the rule it implies — matches are watchable, lobbies are not — is the one you would have chosen anyway.* (2026-07-26) |

| D80 | Half-open sockets are swept with the runtime's OWN keepalive timestamp (P4 online, increment 3c-viii) | The gap 3c-v's browser run surfaced, and the last known way an online match could stall. `setWebSocketAutoResponse` answers the client's 1.5s ping without waking the DO — the thing that makes hibernation worth having — but it also means the DO can never notice pings that STOP. A network that dies without a TCP close therefore left a socket that looked perfectly live forever: `hasSocket` said yes, no disconnect grace was armed, no forfeit clock started, and the opponent waited indefinitely. Every ORDINARY exit (tab close, browser kill, navigation, reload, crash) delivers a close event, which is why this never bit in practice and why it took a deliberate `set offline` to reproduce. **The fix is to read the signal the runtime was already recording:** `state.getWebSocketAutoResponseTimestamp(ws)` returns when it last auto-answered that socket, so liveness costs nothing at ping time. The alternative — dropping the auto-response and stamping `lastSeen` in `webSocketMessage` — was rejected on exactly that ground: it would wake the DO ~40 times a minute per socket and give up hibernation to learn what the runtime already knows. **What it costs instead is a POLL**, because a quiet in-game lobby schedules no alarm at all: `scheduleMaintenance` now pushes a `LIVENESS_SWEEP_MS` (30s) deadline whenever any socket is connected, so an occupied lobby wakes twice a minute. `SOCKET_SILENCE_MS` is 15s — ten missed pings, so a phone changing networks or a throttled background tab is not mistaken for a dead one — and a test pins the budget that matters: sweep + silence + the 5s grace all fit inside the 90s forfeit, so a dead network can never resolve slower than a closed tab. **A `null` timestamp counts as ALIVE**, deliberately: it is the state of every connection before its first ping, and nothing can distinguish "just opened" from "opened long ago and never pinged" — the cost of that reading is a socket we never sweep, versus closing a healthy one-second-old connection. **The sweep only CLOSES**; everything after it — the grace, the seat going disconnected, the forfeit — is the machinery a real close already drives, reached through the same `socketGone` (called explicitly, since a server-side `close()` delivers no `webSocketClose` event to us). 1874 → **1878** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED with the very reproducer that exposed the bug:** with a match live, one client was put offline with the socket left open; before this change that produced nothing at all, and now the opponent saw "disconnected — forfeits in 1:30" after ~50s (15s silence + up to 30s sweep + the 5s grace) and the countdown ran. Bringing the client back online restored it within ~10s — the channel reconnected, re-helloed, and the forfeit was cancelled — while the client that stayed connected throughout was never swept. Zero page errors. *Why: the cheapest signal is the one someone else is already recording. Hibernation exists to avoid waking on keepalives, so any fix that inspects keepalives directly is self-defeating; asking the runtime when it last answered turns a per-ping cost into a per-half-minute one.* (2026-07-26) |

| D81 | A lobby seat's name is the ACCOUNT's, resolved server-side (P5 polish, increment P5-1) | The first P5 item, and the one a player notices before anything else: after twelve P4 increments the lobby still said "Player 6046". A seat's name was whatever the client announced in its `hello` — a handle generated into localStorage, editable by anyone, belonging to nobody. It is now the account's `displayName`, read at the hello by `loadDisplayName` from the same `userId` the /ws session cookie already resolved (the second use of the lobby's one authenticated fact, after the owner-scoped deck read). **Resolving rather than trusting is the decision**, and it is not only cosmetic: the moment names stop being random, a client-supplied name is an impersonation vector — announce yourself as your opponent's handle and the versus screen agrees. The read sits deliberately AFTER the refusal branches, so a socket that gets no seat costs no D1 query however often it retries, and hellos are rare in steady state (heartbeats keep the client's idle timer fresh, so it only re-announces after a real gap). **The fallback rule is the careful part:** a blank account name yields `null`, and the caller drops back to the client's announced handle — never to the EMAIL, which is the one substitution that suggests itself because `AccountButton` uses exactly that. That control shows a user their OWN address; a lobby name is broadcast to the opponent and, since 3c-vii, to spectators. A cosmetic blank is a smaller problem than a published email address, and a test pins it. The client announces its account name as well, which changes nothing authoritative — it only spares the host the half-second where their own seat reads "Player 4271" before the first broadcast corrects it, since the waiting screen is seeded locally. No server-side length cap: registration bounds names at 64, `PlayerPanel` already truncates, and mangling someone's name to fit a panel is the UI's job, not the wire's. 1878 → **1881** tests, `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on four clients:** two signed-in seats read "Ash" and "Misty" across the player panels, the turn banner, the game log ("Ash wins the toss") and the spectator pill ("Watching — Ash vs Misty"), while two SIGNED-OUT players kept their generated handles with the lobby working normally. Zero page errors. *Why: a name is identity, and identity that the client asserts is not identity at all. The server already knew who the socket was — using that costs one query and closes an impersonation hole that only existed because nobody's name meant anything yet.* (2026-07-26) |

| D82 | Generated avatars from a hashed account id, not uploads (P5 polish, increment P5-2) | The other half of "lobby identity", and split from the uploads polish.md describes on purpose: an upload flow means R2, a CSP change, moderation questions and a settings surface, while what the lobby actually lacked was any face at all — `PlayerPanel` drew the same grey figure for everyone. **A generated avatar buys most of the value for none of that infrastructure:** `PlayerState.avatarSeed` carries a token the server derives at the hello from the account behind the socket, and `PlayerAvatar` draws a deterministic two-hue gradient with the account's initial. Nothing is stored, nothing is uploaded, and the property that matters holds — the same person looks the same to you in every lobby, which is what makes it identity rather than decoration (browser-verified across two clients: both saw identical gradients for the same accounts, in opposite seats). **It is a HASH of the account id, never the id.** Not because a uuid is guessable, but because the lobby snapshot is BROADCAST — to the opponent, and since 3c-vii to spectators — and an internal primary key is the wrong thing to publish there: it invites joining this seat to any other surface exposing the same id. FNV-1a is enough because this is a display token, not a security boundary; the client's only use is picking colours. **An anonymous seat gets NO seed and keeps the generic figure**, deliberately: the only id available for one is the per-lobby playerId, and a face derived from that would change on the player's next visit — promising an identity that isn't there is worse than admitting there is none. **Saturation and lightness are fixed**, so the seed picks the hue and never how loud the result is; that is what stops one player's face glowing next to another's in a UI built on restrained glass. An empty name draws a bare gradient rather than a placeholder letter, since a wrong initial is a claim and a gradient is not. The wire field cost one line in `readSnapshot`'s `withSeatDefaults` — the normalizer 3c-v added for exactly this, so a lobby persisted by the previous deploy survives the new field instead of being evicted. 1881 → **1889** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on four clients:** two signed-in seats drew stable, visually distinct faces ("A" orange→amber, "M" cyan→indigo) with byte-identical gradients on both clients, and two signed-out seats kept the generic figure. A screenshot confirmed they sit in-palette against both the blue and red seat washes. *Why: the expensive half of avatars is storage and moderation; the valuable half is that people become recognisable. Deriving a face from the account you already authenticated delivers the second without buying the first, and leaves the upload path a strict improvement rather than a rewrite.* (2026-07-26) |

| D83 | A deck's cover is its highest-HP Pokémon, derived on read (P5 polish, increment P5-3) | The decks grid drew the same blue card back for every deck under a generated tint, while the catalog has had real scans since P2. **The slice's whole content is the RULE**, so it lives in a pure `pickCoverCardId` that can be argued with and tested rather than inside SQL: **the Pokémon with the highest printed HP**, ties broken by copies, then by card id for determinism. HP is the best proxy the catalog offers for "the card this deck is built around", and crucially it is one number rather than a taxonomy to interpret — an ex/V/VMAX or a Stage 2 outranks the Basics that fetch it WITHOUT anyone having to rank `stage` values against each other. The two obvious alternatives both fail on a real deck: ranking by STAGE gets a Basic-ex deck wrong (a 330 HP Basic ex is the payoff and would lose to any Stage 2 tech line), and ranking by COUNT gets nearly every evolution deck wrong (the 4-of Basic beats the 3-of Stage 2 it evolves into — verified live: Skeledirge ×3 correctly beat Fuecoco ×4). Cards with no scan are skipped rather than picked and rendered blank, and a deck with no qualifying card gets `coverCardId: null`, keeping the card back — which is also exactly right for a deck you have only just created (`fromDeck`, the create-response mapper, hardcodes null for that reason). **Derived on READ**, in one extra query for the whole library beside the existing `cardCount` aggregate: no stored column, so a cover follows the deck's contents with nothing to keep in sync, and no write path to change. It is a HEURISTIC and will occasionally pick a wall over the attacker beside it; the proper answer is letting the owner choose, which this leaves room for (a stored choice would simply win over it). **A browser run caught what tests could not:** the deck NAME, white text that had always sat on a flat blue back, was nearly illegible over flames and holo foil — so the label gained a gradient scrim, applied ONLY when there is art, so a coverless deck looks exactly as it did. 1889 → **1899** tests. *Why: the cheapest correct default beats a configuration surface nobody has asked for yet, and HP is the rare heuristic that is both trivially cheap and right on the decks that matter.* (2026-07-26) |

| D84 | The playmat's pass + history controls are DELETED, not relocated (P5 polish, increment P5-4) | The oldest open defect in the repo — "the Pass turn button is unclickable while the turn panel is open", carried since 2026-07-26 with a residual about the history arrows — closed by reading the code rather than the symptom. **Two findings changed the slice entirely.** First, `PreviousButton` and `ForwardButton` **took no props at all**: no `onClick`, no handler, on any surface, ever. They were chrome from the /simulator visual prototype, so the residual ("history navigation stays unreachable during your own turn:action") described navigation that DOES NOT EXIST — and three sessions of docs, mine included, had been treating them as blocked functionality. Two buttons a screen reader announces as "Previous"/"Forward" that do nothing are worse than no buttons: they promise a feature. They are gone, to return WIRED if a real history is ever built. Second, the ⟶ pass control is enabled **exactly when the turn panel renders over it** — the panel appears precisely when `passDisabled === false` — so on /play and online it was never clickable, and relocating it would have been moving a control whose replacement (each panel's own `Pass`) already exists and is browser-verified. **The API expresses the rule instead of a flag:** `PlaymatView.onPassTurn` became OPTIONAL, and the control renders only for a driver that passes one — "give the board a pass control by giving it something to call". The /simulator sandbox passes one (nothing covers it there); /play and online do not, and shed their now-dead `passTurn`/`passDisabled` wiring with it. Rejected: moving the controls to the left edge (the game log already owns bottom-left, and moving DEAD arrows would make them more prominent), and capping the panel's height (a real cost — a long attack/ability list gains a scrollbar — paid to keep a button nobody can use). **Browser-verified at 1280×700 with a real `elementFromPoint` hit-test**, because a synthetic `.click()` is precisely what disguised this bug for weeks: at a live turn:action, /play has no "Pass turn" and no arrows, its in-panel `Pass` hit-tests to itself and advances the turn (TURN 01 → 02); /simulator still has its ⟶, hit-testing to itself. The `verify` skill's entry — which had already been corrected once, from "an agent-browser quirk" to "a real product bug" — is now corrected again to describe the current controls and, more usefully, to state the general rule: eval may DRIVE, but only a hit-test may conclude a control works. 1899 tests, unchanged in count (one test switched from clicking the unreachable ⟶ to the panel's `Pass`). *Why: the fix for a control that cannot be reached is not a better position for it. Once one of the two was never wired and the other was permanently redundant, the honest change was subtraction — and the API that falls out ("pass a handler, get a button") is simpler than the one that was there.* (2026-07-26) |

| D85 | Your account is editable on /settings, and the avatar seed rides on `User` (P5 polish, increment P5-5) | P5-1 made a lobby seat's name the ACCOUNT's `displayName`, resolved server-side and broadcast to the opponent and to spectators — and left a player wearing whatever register picked, with nowhere to change it. **`PATCH /auth/me` + a Profile section on /settings** closes that: one required field (`updateProfileRequestSchema`, trim + 1–64, the same bounds register uses on its OPTIONAL name), `requireUser` BEFORE the validator so a signed-out caller hears "unauthorized" rather than a critique of their body, and no write at all when the name is unchanged. **The interesting decision is the FACE.** A profile that showed a name but no avatar would be a text box calling itself a profile, and one drawing a lookalike avatar would be worse — it would promise a face that isn't the one opponents see. So `userSchema` gained **`avatarSeed`**, derived by the same `avatarSeedFrom` D82 introduced, and the section renders the SAME `PlayerAvatar` the lobby does. That moved two things deliberately: the module to `apps/api/src/auth/avatar.ts` (the seed is a property of the ACCOUNT, not of a seat — lobby already imports from auth, not the reverse) and the component to `src/components/` (shared, so /settings and the lobby cannot drift into two similar drawings). A test pins `avatarSeedOf` against `avatarSeedFrom` for the same reason. **Deriving the seed client-side was rejected**: a second copy of FNV-1a in the web bundle is a hash that must agree with the server forever, and the day it doesn't a player's own face silently stops matching the one everyone else sees. Publishing it costs nothing — the lobby already broadcasts it. Smaller calls: the avatar's initial follows the DRAFT as you type, so the face and the field can never disagree on screen; a 401 on save re-probes the session so the panel becomes the sign-in prompt, which says it better than an error line under a dead form; the form is keyed by `user.id` so switching accounts re-seeds the field. A rename reaches a lobby at the next hello (`loadDisplayName` resolves the seat name there), so a player already seated elsewhere keeps the old name until they rejoin. 1899 → **1911** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two clients:** renaming "Brock" → "Pewter Brock" flipped the initial to "P" live, kept the gradient byte-identical (the seed is the account id, not the name), updated the corner account control with no reload, survived a reload, and — the point of the slice — **the opponent's client showed "Pewter Brock" with that same gradient**. Save and the field both hit-tested to themselves; the signed-out panel offers a sign-in link whose `state.from` returns to /settings. Zero page errors. *Why: an identity you cannot change isn't yours. And once the profile's job is "how you appear to other players", showing anything other than the exact artifacts the lobby broadcasts would make the page a mock-up of itself.* (2026-07-26) |

| D86 | A deck cover is a PIN over the derived default, resolved on read (P5 polish, increment P5-6) | P5-3 fronted every deck with its highest-HP Pokémon and said outright that the proper answer was letting the owner choose. This is that: a nullable `decks.cover_card_id` the editor sets, and a pure **`effectiveCoverCardId(chosen, candidates)`** the list query runs — **the pin wins, and everything else falls through to `pickCoverCardId`**. The derived rule is untouched; it is now explicitly the DEFAULT rather than the answer. **Three decisions carry the slice.** First, **the resolution runs against the candidate list the default was already deriving from**, so honouring a choice costs NO extra query, and a pin that has gone stale (its card removed elsewhere, or its scan gone after a re-ingest) silently falls back instead of drawing a blank rectangle — a deck must never front a card it doesn't run. Second, **the pin may be a TRAINER or an Energy while the derived default stays Pokémon-only**: guessing needs a rule, choosing does not, and a signature Supporter is a legitimate face (browser-verified with Academy at Night, which the rule could never pick). Third, **two fields, deliberately not one**: `DeckSummary.coverCardId` remains the face being DRAWN (so the grid needed no change at all), while the full `Deck` carries `chosenCoverCardId`, the stored CHOICE the editor's picker is set to — null for almost every deck. Collapsing them into one name would have made "what is drawn" and "what was chosen" the same word for two different questions. **`Automatic` is an explicit option in the picker**, not the absence of one, so reverting is one click; it deliberately shows no art, because the default is a RULE and drawing today's answer to it would read as "this card". **The consistency rule lives on both sides:** PATCH validates the pin against the card list the patch LEAVES BEHIND (400 `cover card not in deck`) and drops a pin whose card that patch removes, while the editor's `toSnapshot` drops it locally too — not cosmetic, since the cover rides the same debounced PATCH as the card list, and a self-contradicting body would 400 forever (the saver retries the same dirty snapshot). The cover joining that one save path is what keeps the pair atomic. 1911 → **1932** tests; migration `0004`. **BROWSER-VERIFIED end to end at 1920×1080:** pinning the Trainer put it on the library tile, removing it dropped the pin, kept saving ("Saved", PATCH 200 — the wedge that would have followed a naive implementation) and fell back to Absol; a pin survived a reload through GET /decks/:id; a hand-crafted PATCH naming a card outside the deck got the 400; and both the footer control and a picker tile hit-tested to themselves. *Why: a heuristic that is right most of the time plus an override that is always right beats trying to make the heuristic cleverer — and the override is only trustworthy if the two can never disagree about what the deck contains.* (2026-07-26) |
| D87 | A favourite deck is a validated PIN of the account's OWN deck, resolved on read (P5 polish, increment P5-7) | Joins P5-5 (an editable profile) and P5-6 (a deck's cover art): an account pins one of its own decks as its favourite and sees that deck's cover + name on /settings. A nullable **`users.favourite_deck_id`** (migration `0005`) surfaced as **`userSchema.favouriteDeckId`** — **just the ID.** **Three choices.** First, **store only the id, not the deck's name/cover:** /settings resolves those from the caller's own `GET /decks` list, so a rename or re-cover shows through with no stale copy and a favourite whose deck has vanished resolves to a blank tile — the SAME "derive on read, tolerate stale" the covers use (D83/D86). Second, **a favourite must be one of YOUR decks:** `PATCH /auth/me` gains an OPTIONAL nullable `favouriteDeckId` (absent leaves it, null clears it, a string is validated against the caller's decks) and 400s `favourite deck not found` for anyone else's id or a phantom — the account can never point at a deck it doesn't own, mirroring D86's `cover card not in deck`. Third, **the FK is `ON DELETE SET NULL`** so deleting a favourited deck clears the pin rather than blocking the delete (a RESTRICT FK would) or dangling a stale id; D1 enforces FKs here (the deck DELETE leans on `deck_cards`' cascade), and since drizzle-kit drops the referential action on an ADD COLUMN, migration 0005 restores `ON DELETE SET NULL` by hand. The users↔decks mutual reference forced an `AnySQLiteColumn` annotation to break the TS type cycle. The picker is a native `<select>` in the SAME form as the name, riding ONE PATCH so name + favourite save atomically; the provider adopts the answer, so the corner control follows with no re-probe. 1932 → **1936** tests. Browser-verified end to end 2026-07-27 (build #71): pin on /settings → cover (`sv06.5-012`, real art) + name render + `PATCH /auth/me` 200; survives reload; `DELETE`ing the deck clears `favouriteDeckId` to null server-side (the FK) and the tile goes blank. Also covered by a DOM round-trip test + schema units. *Why: the profile only needs to REFERENCE a deck, and the deck already owns its name and cover — copying them onto the account would just be a second source of truth to keep in sync; the id plus resolve-on-read is the whole feature, and the validated pin + SET NULL make "your favourite is always a real deck of yours" true by construction.* (2026-07-27) |
| D88 | A card detail view is a read-only modal hydrated on demand from the full-card endpoint (P5 polish, increment P5-8) | Cards in the deck builder had only ever been an add-a-copy tile; this adds a **detail modal** opened from the grid that shows the full card (attacks, abilities, weakness/resistance/retreat, illustrator, rarity, regulation mark, Trainer/Energy rules text). The backend was already complete — a `GET /cards/:id` returning the full `cardSchema`, and a battle-tested `getCard` client — so the whole slice is web-UI; nothing in API/DB/schema/ingest changed. **Four decisions.** First, **the inspect affordance is a SIBLING of the add button, never a child:** the grid tile is a `<button>` (click adds, right-click removes, pointer-down drags) and a button-in-button is invalid HTML, so a corner info button floats over the tile at `z-20`, revealed on tile hover and its own keyboard focus; clicking it opens the modal and does NOT add a copy, and the three existing tile gestures are untouched. Second, **seed + fetch, and the fetch must never blank the seed:** the header name and big image come from the `BuilderCard` the grid already holds (up the instant the dialog opens) while the body swaps loading→ready as `getCard` resolves; a `cancelled` flag drops a stale response so reopening a different card can't be clobbered by a slower earlier fetch, and the seed is retained through the close so nothing flashes empty. Third, **honest about what the catalog stores:** pricing, national-dex number and flavor text are NOT in D1 (see the `cards` schema header), so the view doesn't show them, and the set prints as raw `{localId} · {setId}` rather than a name lookup the payload can't back; every block is guarded so a Trainer/Energy omits what it lacks. Fourth, **the new surface respects `prefers-reduced-motion`** (`motion-reduce:transition-none`, no scale under reduced motion) — a down-payment on the reduced-motion-audit item, and the model the older P5 surfaces (CoverDialog, PlayerAvatar) still need. Type/cost pips reuse `EnergyGlyph` behind a pure exported `isGlyphType` guard that text-chips anything outside the ten glyph keys. 1936 → **1941** tests (a jsdom dialog test + the guard). Built via sub-agents; main session reviewed the diff — self-reviewed, no independent reviewer. **BROWSER-VERIFIED end to end 2026-07-27 (build #72):** hover revealed the corner button; clicking "View Absol details" opened the modal (name instant) then `GET /cards/:id` (200) filled Attacks/Combat/Illus + a high-quality `<img>` (`naturalWidth=600`) from the R2 mirror; the info click left "Add Absol" at 0-in-deck; a Trainer refetched to show only its Effect; X/Escape/backdrop all closed it; zero console errors. *Why: the data already existed one endpoint away — the only real question was interaction, and making inspect a peer of add (not a mode on it) keeps the fast add-a-copy loop intact while giving the card a place to be read; hydrate-on-open with a stale-guard is the same "derive on read" discipline the rest of P5 uses, and refusing to invent pricing/set-name keeps the view trustworthy.* (2026-07-27) |
| D89 | Carry `prefers-reduced-motion` through the remaining new-P5 surfaces by appending `motion-reduce:*` variants AT THE CALL SITE (P5 polish, increment P5-9) | Three new P5 surfaces still animated unconditionally, and the app's ONLY global reduced-motion rule (`src/index.css` L190) neutralises just the `<body>` glow transition and the `.route-view` route-fade — it never touches a Tailwind `transition-*`/`scale-*`/`animate-*` utility on a component, so those surfaces were unguarded. An Explore audit found the offenders on `CoverDialog.tsx` (`TILE_BASE`'s `hover:scale-[1.03] active:scale-95`, the close button's `transition-colors`, the Done ghost button), `Settings.tsx` (the font `<select>`, the back-arrow icon's `transition-transform` slide, the Back-to-home ghost button) and `ProfileSection.tsx` (the Save button, the sign-in link); **`PlayerAvatar` was already clean** (its only `style` is a static gradient). Each got `motion-reduce:transition-none` appended, matching the **D88 idiom exactly**: the P5-8 author deliberately patched at the CALL SITE rather than editing the shared `glass.ts` constants, and this slice honours that placement (the alternative — guarding the constants — is written up as the next resume point, see below). **One judgement call:** the Save spinner is a perpetual `animate-spin`, which `transition-none` cannot stop, so it got **`motion-reduce:animate-none`** — the one genuinely-new variant this codebase hadn't used. Halting the spin is the correct accessible behaviour: the "Save" text plus the disabled/dimmed button already convey the in-progress state, and `SpinnerIcon` is a 3/4-ring arc that reads fine frozen. Pure CSS-class slice — no logic, component, or API/DB/schema touch; 1941 → 1941 tests (nothing new to unit-test: `motion-reduce:*` variants aren't observable in jsdom and asserting className strings is brittle). `bun run check` green (137 files / 1941 tests); engine rules unchanged (0.41.0). **Verified via the shipped stylesheet, not a browser drive:** a production `vite build` emits BOTH `motion-reduce:transition-none` and `motion-reduce:animate-none` inside `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)`, proving the guards are live and not a silently-dropped typo'd variant — the one real failure mode for a class-only change; a full browser drive was judged low-value since the change is purely additive + reduced-motion-only (it cannot regress the default render) and the `transition-none` mechanism is already browser-verified from P5-8. **Deliberately deferred (the D89 resume point):** the ROOT CAUSE — the shared `glass.ts` button constants (`GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_ACCENT_BUTTON`, `GLASS_HUD_BUTTON`, `GLASS_NEUTRAL_BUTTON`, `GLASS_DIALOG_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_FILTER_TOGGLE`, `GLASS_INPUT`) all carry `transition-*` + `active:scale-95` with no reduced-motion guard and feed EVERY glass button app-wide, so the same defect still lives outside the P5 surfaces; adding `motion-reduce:transition-none` INTO those constants would fix the whole app in one additive edit, but it reverses this decision's call-site placement and reaches well beyond the P5 scope, so it's a deliberate app-wide pass worth the user's nod. *Why: the resume point scoped this to the three P5 surfaces and D88 set the call-site precedent; honouring that keeps the slice small, low-risk and non-reversing, while the root-cause `glass.ts` fix is flagged loudly as the natural, bigger follow-up rather than silently smuggled into a "P5 surfaces" slice.* **REVERSED by D90 (P5-10) — the constant-level guard was done and the call-site placement is no longer the precedent for these constants.** (2026-07-27) |
| D90 | Guard `prefers-reduced-motion` at the SOURCE — put `motion-reduce:transition-none` INTO the shared `glass.ts` constants, reversing D88/D89's call-site placement (P5 polish, increment P5-10) | The root cause D89 flagged and wrote up as its resume point, now fixed. The seven `glass.ts` constants that carry a transition — `GLASS_HUD_BUTTON`, `GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_DIALOG_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_ACCENT_BUTTON`, `GLASS_NEUTRAL_BUTTON`, `GLASS_FILTER_TOGGLE` (all `transition-all` + `active:scale-95`) and `GLASS_INPUT` (`transition-colors`) — each got `motion-reduce:transition-none` appended, so EVERY glass control app-wide (auth, lobby HUD, filters, decks, dialogs, corner controls) respects reduced motion from the constant rather than each call site; one additive edit covers the whole app. Under reduced motion the fade is off and the `active:scale-95` SNAPS rather than animating — the same behaviour `CoverDialog`'s `TILE_BASE` was browser-verified with in P5-8, so this is not a new interaction, only a wider one. **This REVERSES D88/D89's "patch at the call site" placement** for these constants; the reversal is deliberate and was named as the blessed next step in D89's own resume point, so it is a trailed reversal, not a silent one. A header comment in `glass.ts` now states the invariant ("every constant with a `transition-*` also carries `motion-reduce:transition-none`") so the next author keeps new transitional constants guarded. **Because the constant now supplies the guard, the four call-site guards that sat adjacent to a glass constant became redundant and were dropped:** `CardDetailDialog` Retry (`GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`), `Settings` Back-to-home (`GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`), `CoverDialog` Done (`GLASS_DIALOG_GHOST_BUTTON`), `ProfileSection` Save (`GLASS_ACCENT_BUTTON`). **Every call-site guard protecting an OWN non-constant transition was KEPT** — `Settings` font `<select>` + back-arrow icon slide, `CoverDialog` `TILE_BASE` + close button, `CardDetailDialog` close button, `CardGrid` info button, `ProfileSection` sign-in link, and the Save spinner's `motion-reduce:animate-none` (a `transition-none` cannot stop an `animate-spin`). Pure CSS-class slice — no logic/component/API/DB/schema touch; 1941 → 1941 tests (nothing new to unit-test: `motion-reduce:*` isn't observable in jsdom and className assertions are brittle). `bun run check` green (137 files / 1941 tests); engine rules unchanged (0.41.0). **Verified via the shipped stylesheet, not a browser drive:** a production `vite build` emits `.motion-reduce\:transition-none{transition-property:none}` (beside `.motion-reduce\:animate-none`) inside `@media (prefers-reduced-motion:reduce)` in the main `index-*.css` bundle — the shared chunk where the glass buttons live — proving the guard is live, not a silently-dropped typo'd variant. A browser drive was judged low-value: the change is purely additive + reduced-motion-only (cannot regress the default render), the mechanism is browser-verified from P5-8, and the constant-vs-call-site placement is invisible in the compiled CSS (cascade is by stylesheet order, not class-attribute order, so moving the class from the call site into the constant string cannot change the result). *Why: D89 deliberately chose the small call-site slice and flagged the root-cause constant fix as the natural follow-up "worth the user's nod (or a considered autonomous slice)"; the autoloop resume point picked it up as that considered slice. Fixing the source and dropping the now-redundant per-call-site guards leaves ONE place the reduced-motion guard lives for glass controls, which is more maintainable than N call sites drifting out of sync — the exact reason these constants exist.
